## **Corporate Hybrids Playbook** 

## **Staying in Short Calls, Revisiting Call Risks** 

Following a particularly weak 2018, where corporate hybrids experienced their worst total returns in a decade, the asset class has experienced a rebound in January. Hybrid performance has matched HY YTD (p3). 

We like short-call paper and avoid risk layering. Gappy price action in periods of stress has increased focus on call risks. We believe that both issuers focused on ratings and more tactical users will execute timely calls. The remaining rated 2019 call pipeline is a modest €4.1bln while hybrids reaching first call dates in 2020 are worth a more meaningful €13bln (p4-5). 

On the supply front, our forecast for 2019 is €20-25bln across all currencies versus issuance of €20bln in 2018 (p6). 

While relative valuations look slightly more attractive compared to seniors, hybrid spreads appear less compelling versus BBs. We screen for recent underperformers (p7). 

## **Hybrid Monitor and Features ‘Cheat Sheet’** 

We track relative value within European corporate hybrids (p9) and highlight key structures and terms of each security (p23).

**M O R G A N   S T A N L E Y   R E S E A R C H Corporate Hybrids Playbook** February 4, 2019 

## **2018 Performance Review and the Year Ahead** 

- 2018 was a challenging year for European credit, with the asset class experiencing the worst performance since 2011. Corporate hybrids fared particularly poorly, with their underperformance versus BBs running contrary to our expectations. Last year, the iBoxx EUR IG non-fin sub index generated total returns of -4.4%, the lowest in a decade. What became apparent as the year progressed was that the narrower investor base for hybrids translated into a significantly larger liquidity premium in the risk off environment. 

- Looking ahead, we stick with the bear market playbook for credit and are not embracing the recent rally as valuations are back to pre-sell-off levels, with a majority of the move happening in a relatively illiquid market (see A Reluctant Rally). While we do not anticipate a similar magnitude of spread widening in 2019 compared to 2018, we believe that investors should improve the quality and liquidity of their portfolios. Within hybrids, we maintain an exposure to short-call IG-rated paper, and avoid layering risks. 

- We note that the improved tone last month allowed issuers to tap the market, with three deals worth €2.5bln pricing in the final two weeks of January. This marked the busiest start to the year since 2014.

**M O R G A N   S T A N L E Y   R E S E A R C H Corporate Hybrids Playbook** February 4, 2019 

## **Exploring Call Risk in Corporate Hybrids** 

- **Issuers focused on ratings preservation likely to call on time:** Almost all European corporate hybrids will lose their S&P equity credit at their first call date (see Call Risks in Corporate Hybrids). Therefore, issuers who want to retain rating agency capital benefit have a large incentive to exercise timely calls. The main exceptions are hybrids from sub-IG issuers and those rated by other agencies, as they will likely retain equity credit beyond the first call date. 

- **For more tactical issuers, call economics will likely be driven by competitiveness of hybrids as a funding instrument:** After the first call date, hybrids would be similar to senior bonds in S&P’s leverage calculations. So, for issuers less committed to hybrids in their capital structure, the comparison that matters is the reset spread on hybrids versus the cost of new long-dated senior funding. The average coupon spread on EUR hybrids issued in 2017/18 was ~240bp, well wide of the 109bp spread on the iBoxx long-dated senior index. Calls are therefore economical even for this subset of issuers, but there are some exceptions. 

- **Where we see extension risks:** 1) Hybrids with low at-issue spreads versus current seniors, 2) Bonds rated by agencies other than S&P, and 3) Instruments from sub-IG issuers face higher extension risks.

## **2019 Call Pipeline and Non-Call Events (Intentional and Otherwise)** 

- We estimate that six rated hybrids worth €4.1bln and 12 worth €13bln will reach their first call dates in 2019 and 2020, respectively. Currently all of these bonds are trading to their respective first call dates. Away from this cohort we note that the ENGIFP £4.625 hybrid was already called on time in early January, the COFP €4.87 was not called and the ENELIM €6.5 was also not called on time due to a “procedural issue”. 

- **Fourth non-call in European corporate hybrids…** The non-call of the Casino €4.87 bond marks the fourth rated hybrid which was not called at its first call date (see Call Risks in Corporate Hybrids for a discussion of past extension events). The issuer has a prior history of not executing timely hybrid calls as it left a perp outstanding in 2010. 

- **…though likely to be an exception:** It is important to note that unlike the vast majority of the rated hybrid universe, the Casino hybrid did not lose its equity credit at the first call date. Under S&P's criteria, hybrids from HY issuers retain their equity content for longer than their IG counterparts. In this instance, S&P has stated that “[the] hybrid would likely lose its intermediate equity content in 2024, since we consider the intermediate equity content on the hybrid will be maintained for an additional five years after the 2019 first call date given Casino’s downgrade to speculative grade in March 2016”. In addition, the call economics were unattractive from the issuer’s perspective since the hybrid reset coupon, currently almost 4%, is actually lower than the yield on its 2026 senior bond (~5.5%). 

- **The ‘accidental’ extension:** Enel’s failure to call on time, even though the issuer refinanced the bond in 2018, has provided a potential windfall to existing holders since the market price subsequently rose to ~107 from close to par. The rally was due to the relatively large reset spread (524bp) and the fact that it cannot be called for another five years. The issuer tried to tender for the bond at par in early January, though the take-up was minimal (€5mln).

## **Expecting Modestly Higher Supply in 2019** 

- **Overall, we forecast 2019 hybrid supply of €20-€25bln across all currencies versus €20bln in 2018:** Looking across the drivers: 

- **Refinancing:** Most of the 2019 call pipeline has already been refinanced, but we expect a high proportion of the 2020 callable hybrids to be refinanced this year. This is likely to contribute ~€10bln in gross issuance. 

- **M&A:** Following Takeda’s purchase of Shire, the company announced that part of the acquisition will be financed using hybrid capital. Supply from Bayer and a potential top-up from Vodafone also cannot be ruled out. While incremental M&A appetite may be lower, we would anticipate up to €6bln of merger-driven supply. 

- **Capital structure optimisation:** This has not been a particularly strong motivator in the past. However, EDP cited a combination of maturity extension, credit support and financial flexibility as key benefits of its recent hybrid issuance. We screen for issuers based on the following methodology: 

   - ‘Hybrid Limit’ using lower of S&P/Moody’s guidance. 

   - ‘Cost of 7Y Debt’ interpolated from € cash curve where available, otherwise uses average 6.5-7.5yr for same ratings bucket and sector.

**M O R G A N   S T A N L E Y   R E S E A R C H Corporate Hybrids Playbook** February 4, 2019 

## **Revisiting Hybrid Relative Value and Screening for Underperformers** 

- The chart on the right simply plots IG hybrid spreads against IG non-fin seniors. It suggests that, at current senior spread levels, hybrids appear modestly cheap compared to history. At the same time, a comparison of hybrids to non-fin BBs suggests that relative valuations are not particularly compelling. In our 2019 outlook, we turned neutral on hybrids versus BBs, given the greater downside beta exhibited by the former. 

**Hybrids Screen Slightly Cheap Compared to Senior Spreads…**

**‘YTC ∆ to Non-Call ‘Kink’** – indicates yield increase needed for hybrid to reach point where it no longer trades to first call, i.e., spread change to take hybrid from point A to point C. A lower spread indicates hybrid is more vulnerable to negative convexity. 

**‘Estimated ∆ in Dur’** – indicates the potential change in hybrid duration when yields rise by 100bp, i.e., duration at point B less duration at point A. **Increase in duration indicates negative convexity.**

Corresponding bullet senior spreads interpolated from senior unsecured cash curve if it exists in the corresponding currency, CDS curve adjusted for basis if not. ‘Upside/Downside’ refers to the ratio of price return from 100bp tightening in yields to price return from 100bp widening in yields. ‘YTC ∆ to Non-Call Kink’ refers to yield increase needed to make hybrid no longer trade to first call. For 3M Ranges, bars highlighted in red shows hybrids where price at >80% range highs, or spreads at <20% range tights. Bars in green show hybrids where price at <20% range lows, or spreads at >80% range wides. **Source:** Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

## **MARKET OVERVIEW** 

## **How Much Issuance Has There Been? How Bi Is the Investable Universe? g** 

## **Strong issuance since 2012** 

- Hybrid issuance surged in 2013, with €30 billion of supply, up from €5 billion in 2012. The strong supply continued in 2014, with over €33 billion of supply. 2015 was another strong year, with just under €33 billion issued. 

- 2016 saw much more limited primary activity, given the market volatility, leading to less than €9bln being issued. Since then volumes have gradually increased with 2017 seeing a pick-up to just under €15bln and 2018 supply rising further to €20bln.

EXAMPLES ON THIS SLIDE ARE FOR ILLUSTRATIVE PURPOSES ONLY **MARKET OVERVIEW** 

## **Wh Do Cor orates Issue H brids? y p y** 

## **Counted as 50% equity by rating agencies** 

- Due to their subordination and potential for coupon deferral, hybrids are typically accorded 50% equity content by rating agencies in the calculations of financial ratios. 

## **Counted as 100% equity by under IFRS** 

- If a hybrid has no dated maturity, it is treated as 100% equity throughout its life under IFRS. Coupons are included in financing cash flow, and are not expensed. 

## **Tax-deductible** 

- Interest costs on hybrids are typically taxdeductible across European countries. 

## **Lowers leverage, improves WACC** 

- Assuming the hypothetical issuer shown on the right replaces a portion of debt with hybrid, WACC increases marginally to 7.2%, but 

   - ‘leverage’, as measured by debt/equity, falls from 0.7x to 0.3x. 

- Assuming the hypothetical issuer shown on the right replaces a portion of equity with hybrid, due to the tax shield from corporate perps, WACC declines to 6.1% even as debt/equity remains unchanged.

**TERMS & STRUCTURE What Are the T ical Structural Features of a Cor orate H brid? yp p y** 

## **Coupon and maturity structure** 

- Hybrids issued since 2012 generally have a fixed coupon until the first call date (often five to seven years from issuance). 

- If bonds are not called at this point, the coupon generally resets to a swaps-linked coupon, with another call date and reset five years down the road (i.e., ~10 years from issuance). 

## **Coupon deferrals** 

- In almost all cases, coupons can be deferred at the option of the company. 

- Skipped coupons typically accrue cumulatively, and generally must be paid in full – sometimes with interest – as a prerequisite for paying a dividend on common or preferred stock. 

## **Changing European Hybrid Coupon Structures**

## **TERMS & STRUCTURE** 

## **How Are H brids Treated b Ratin A encies? What Is E uit Credit? y y g g q y** 

Rating agencies tend to treat hybrid securities as part-debt, part-equity in their financial ratio calculations. But this ‘equity credit’ lasts only as long as the hybrid possess equity-like features 

## **Dated Hybrids** 

- **S&P** – Hybrid loses its 50% equity credit _20 years before the ‘effective maturity’_ (the call date at which the cumulative step-up reaches 100bp or higher). 

## **Perps** 

   - **S&P** – Hybrid loses its 50% equity credit _20 years before the ‘effective maturity’_ (the call date at which the cumulative step-up reaches 100bp or higher). 

- **Fitch** – Hybrid is considered 100% debt _5 years before the ‘effective maturity’_ (the call date at which the cumulative step-up _**exceeds**_ 100bp). 

- **Moody’s** – Hybrid loses equity benefit _10 years before the stated maturity of the hybrid._ 

- **Fitch** – Hybrid keeps equity content for life (as long as cumulative step-up does not exceed 100bp). 

- **Moody’s** – Hybrid keep equity content for life (unless issuer is HY) _._ 

## **Equity Credit Treatment for Dated IG Hybrids**

## **TERMS & STRUCTURE – SPECIAL EVENT CALLS What Are S ecial Event Calls? p** 

Special Event Calls give issuers the option to redeem a hybrid early, usually when the perp no longer functions as intended at issuance. These include: 

## **Rating Event Call/Capital Event Call** 

- These give issuers the option to redeem a hybrid early **,** _**if the equity benefit of the instrument allowed by rating agencies deteriorates (subject to conditions)**_ 

- Calls are dependent on equity content, _NOT_ hybrid or issuer rating (at least not directly) 

## **Accounting Event Calls** 

- Accounting Event call gives issuers the option to redeem if a hybrid is no longer classified as “equity” or “financial liability” under IFRS, or the applicable accounting standards. The majority of hybrids with this type of call are redeemable at 101. 

## **Change-of-Control Calls** 

- If not called after a CoC Event, the coupon would step up immediately by 500bp. 

- What investors should be aware of is what constitutes a CoC Event. As the name suggests, a CoC Event generally comprises: 

   - some sort of change in ownership – the exact definition of which would typically be with reference to takeover laws of the issuers’ domestic jurisdiction, _**AND**_ 

   - a ratings downgrade or withdrawal, within a certain window from the CoC, that is the result of the CoC; if a deterioration in rating is unconnected to the CoC, then the CoC call would not be triggered. 

## **Tax Event Calls (Gross-Up/Tax Event)** 

- Gross-Up Event call (or Withholding Tax Event) gives issuers the option to redeem early if they have to pay additional amounts to hybrid holders to make up for any taxes withheld or deducted due to changes in tax legislation. 

- Tax Event call gives issuers the option to call if cost on hybrids are no longer tax-deductible, due to changes in tax legislation. 

## **Clean-Up Calls** 

- These allow issuers the option to redeem when the amount outstanding of a hybrid drops below a certain level, typically about 10-25% of the amount originally issued.

**M O R G A N   S T A N L E Y   R E S E A R C H Corporate Hybrids Playbook** 

February 4, 2019 

## **TERMS & STRUCTURE – SPECIAL EVENT CALLS Case Stud : 2015 U date from S&P y p** 

- **What happened?** On October 27, 2015, S&P revised the equity content it assigns to some hybrid issues from “intermediate” to “minimal”. In all, 29 hybrid issues from 14 issuers were affected. As a result, these hybrids were fully treated as debt and lost all equity benefit received from S&P. For a discussion on why this occurred, please see _Dealing with S&P’s Surprise_ (October 29, 2017). 

- **How did issuers respond?** Over time, all the affected companies bar one issued undertakings partially waiving their call rights. This option allowed them to regain equity benefit at S&P while at the same time avoiding payment of a consent fee to investors. For these names, S&P reinstated “intermediate” equity content. Rexam did not appear to issue a waiver, though its affected hybrid was called following the company’s acquisition by Ball in July 2016. 

- **Implications for investors:** Price action in the names where issuers have opted for waiving call rights has been negative. Consent fees would have clearly been a source of some upside for investors. However, given that the call option has always been with the issuer, it is not obvious to us if investors had a strong case to push back on the modifications anyway. In our view, further downside associated with any future waivers should be limited.

## **TERMS & STRUCTURE – SPECIAL EVENT CALLS What Has Been the Ex erience of Ratin Event Calls? p g** 

**Change in rating criteria example – DONGAS €7.75** 

**Issuer junked by Moody’s example – TITIM € 7.75** 

- **Tender price: 104** 

- **REC call price: 101** 

- **Hybrid price before tender/call: 110** 

- S&P’s change in methodology in April 2013 eroded the security’s equity benefit, triggering the REC 

- Hybrid was eventually tendered and exchanged at 104 in June 2013 

**Issuer rated HY by Moody’s example – WIEAV €6.5** 

**Final outcomes for hybrid investors have varied** 

         - **Tender price: n/a** 

      - **REC call price: 101** 

   - **Hybrid price before tender/call: 103** 

- When TITIM became HY in October 2013, its 

- € 7.75 hybrid lost its equity content, triggering the REC; but perp was trading below its REC 

- call price at the time, was not economical to call 

- Given there’s no time limit to when the call 

- could be exercised, TITIM chose to redeem the hybrid in January 2014, when the perp was trading above 101 

**Issuer upgraded by S&P example – ALLRNV € 4.875** 

- **Tender price: n/a** 

- **REC call price: Makewhole** 

- **Hybrid price before tender/call: n/a** 

- The hybrid lost all equity benefit following Moody’s new methodology in July 2013. 

- Because the hybrid had makewhole call, it was uneconomical for the company to redeem before the first call date. 

- The hybrid has been left outstanding, with little impact on the price. 

      - **Tender price: 102.5** 

      - **REC call price: 101** 

   - **Hybrid price before tender/call: 104** 

- Hybrid’s RCC became void post S&P’s _upgrade_ of ALLRNV to AA- in August 2013. 

- At the time the ALLRNV perps were issued in 2010, S&P methodology required an RCC to mitigate the 100bp step-up in order for the security to receive 

- equity benefit. With the RCC inactive, equity benefit could be withdrawn, triggering a Rating Event Call at 101.

**Source:** Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

February 4, 2019 

## **TERMS & STRUCTURE What Are Re lacement Ca ital Covenants/Re lacement Intention Lan ua e? p p p g g** 

**Replacement Capital Covenant (RCC) typically states that issuer could only redeem hybrids with funds from newly raised hybrid or common stock** 

- Most European hybrids would carry a non-legally enforceable form of replacement language, which we think offers little analytical purpose or differentiation. 

## **Replacement Language – When It Takes Force Matters** 

## **A) Replacement Language From Issuance** 

**Call Date** 

**FIXED COUPON Margin + Swap** ~~Pp~~ Replacement language in force. If hybrid called at call date, issuer would have to fund redemption with hybrid issuance or capital raising. 

- While RCC is in force, corporates have an obligation to their senior creditors to preserve credit quality, such that in the face of potential calls, they must either shore up their capital base, or simply choose not to call. 

 **AKIN TO PERMANENT CAPITAL** 

## **B) Replacement Language After First Call Date Call Date** ! 

**FIXED COUPON Margin + Swap** 

## **RCC at issuance – akin to permanent capital** 

- Signal of intent on the issuer’s part to keep the hybrid as permanent capital. 

##  **INCENTIVE TO CALL AT CALL DATE** 

Replacement language in force. If hybrid called _after_ call date, issuer would have to fund redemption with hybrid issuance or capital raising. 

Source: Morgan Stanley Research 

## **RCC after first call date – incentive to call** 

- RCC became an incentive for companies to call at the first call date, before the covenant takes force and replacing the hybrids become more expensive.

## **HYBRID CALLS – DRIVERS, FEATURES & CASE STUDIES What Has Been the Track Record for Calls/Non-Calls?** 

## **2017 and 2018 saw all calls being exercised on time, but precedent for non-call exists** 

- The European hybrids universe is still relatively nascent. 

- All rated hybrids eligible to be called in 2017 and 2018 have been called at their first call date (26 bonds in total). 

- In 2015, all nine hybrids that were eligible to be called that year were called, apart from one anticipated exception (SZUGR 2.968%). 

- One example of a non-call was TUIGR in 2013. It is a high yield Leisure and Travel company with a very different profile from the issuers in the European hybrid universe now. Its hybrid was subsequently called in 2015. 

## **Potential Calls Increasing in Coming Years**

## **HYBRID CALLS – DRIVERS, FEATURES & CASE STUDIES Which Factors Drive an Issuer’s Decision to Call or Not Call at the First Call Date?** 

The financial ability of a company to retire debt trumps all, but hybrid structure and language can also play significant roles in influencing an issuer’s decision to call or not to call at the first call date. 

**Loss of equity content** 

**What?** Rating agencies treat portion of hybrid as equity. But this benefit may fall away after a period of time, creating incentive for issuers to call. 

**Useful for valuating:** Hybrids issued post-2012. 

**Hybrids more likely to get called if:** Equity benefit typically falls away at the first call date. This is the case for nearly all European hybrids issued since 2012. European hybrids could see their equity content intact after the first call date. 

**Coupon resets and step-ups** 

**What?** If not called at the first call date, the coupon typically resets to a Libor or swaps-linked coupon, usually L + margin where the margin equals the spread at issuance. 

**Useful for valuating:** All vintages of hybrids. 

**Hybrids more likely to get called if:** Spread at which issuer can refinance is lower than the step-up + spread of hybrid at issuance. 

**Replacement language/ replacement capital covenant (RCC)** 

**What?** Replacement language generally requires issuers to redeem the existing hybrid only with funds from newly raised hybrid or common stock. 

**Useful for valuating:** All hybrids, although style of replacement language varies by vintage. 

**Hybrids more likely to get called if:** Legally enforceable RCC exists, and takes effect after the first call date; _or_ to a lesser extent, replacement language/intention exists, and takes effect after the first call date.

## **HYBRID VALUATIONS How Should Investors Value Cor orate H brids? p y** 

We decompose an illustrative hybrid’s yield-to-worst (YTW) into different components: 

## **Rates Risk** 

- Swap rate interpolated to the YTW date. 

## **Senior Credit Risk** 

- The compensation investors get for taking on bullet, non-deferrable, senior unsecured credit risk. 

- Cash spreads where available, CDS adjusted for basis where not. 

## **Subordination Pick-Up** 

- Extra compensation for lower recovery. 

- Subordinated risk pick-up ≈ senior spreads/(1-35%) less senior spread. 

## **Hybrid Compensation** 

- YTW less rates risk, senior credit risk and subordination pick-up. 

- Spread an investor receives for hybrid-specific risks such as coupon deferability and uncertain cash flows. 

**Given two perps with exactly the same features and terms, the one with the higher hybrid compensation should be more attractive for investors.**

## **What Is H brid Com ensation? What Kind of Risks Are Investors Bein Paid For? y p g** 

Hybrid compensation should cover investors for the following risks: 

**Illustrative Example - ENGIFP* € 3.875% Hybrid YTW Breakdown** 

## **Coupon Deferral Risk** 

- Hybrid coupons can be suspended in a period of extreme stress, yet this is a relatively small risk in terms of likelihood of occurrence or the impact on yields. 

- Hybrid coupons are generally deferrable and cumulative. For many perps, the deferred coupons also bear interest such that the economic impact on bond yields for issuer or investors if coupons are deferred is minimal. 

## **Duration Extension Risk** 

- Hybrids can be redeemed on specified dates at the option of issuers, meaning that investors are exposed to potential non-call risk. 

## **Special Event Calls Risk** 

- Apart from non-call risk, hybrid investors also suffer from _early_ call risk – Special Event Calls allow issuers to redeem a hybrid early under certain circumstances, before its first call date, capping potential upside.

## **In effect, hybrid compensation is the spread investors receive for selling a number of coupon deferral and early call options to the issuer.**

## **What Ha ens to Cor orate H brids if Yields Rise? pp p y** 

**Rise in hybrid yield for perps trading to call would see price fall – but impact is similar to that of yield move on bullet bond with same coupon and maturity equal to hybrid’s call date** 

- For hybrids trading to their call dates, and where YTC is far away from YTM, a 100bp rise in hybrid yield will shift the perp along the YTW curve from Point A to Point B. 

- In this example, price falls by about 4 points, but the hybrid continues to trade to call (that is, YTW=YTC). 

- Note that this shift has basically the same impact as a 100bp rise in yield of a bullet bond with the same coupon, with a maturity equal to the hybrid’s call date.

**What Ha ens to Cor orate H brids if Rates Ex ectations Rise? pp p y p** 

## **Rising Rates Expectations Push YTM Higher, Leaving More Headroom Before Duration Extension** 

**Rise in rates expectations benefits hybrid investors as it pushes out YTW, meaning it would take a larger increase in hybrid yield until perp starts to see duration extend** 

- Suppose now that we expect the 5y swap rate at the hybrid’s first call date is going to increase by 100bp. 

- As coupon payments after the first call date are indexed off the 5y swap rate at the first call date in this example, such a rate move would impact the yield to the second call date, the third call date and maturity. 

- The Exhibits on the right show how a rise in swap rates can move the yield to second call (YT2C). YT2C increases at every price point because future cash flows (coupons indexed to swap rates) have increased. 

- The cumulative impact of this is that YTW is pushed outwards, and that it **would take a larger increase in hybrid yield for the corporate perp to start suffering from negative convexity.**

**HYBRID VALUATIONS How Does Ne ative Convexit Relate to H brids? g y y** 

**Potential early redemption means a hybrid’s upside can be capped, while risk of maturity extension can see duration increase as yields rise** 

- A hybrid’s callability means that some of the upside may be capped – duration basically falls as yield falls to ‘extreme’ lows. 

- On the other hand, as yields rise, the ‘worst’ yield for a hybrid will shift from its YTC to its YTM. Since the latter represents a much longer effective date, it means duration rises as yields rise to ‘extremes’. 

- On the right, both Hybrid A and B trade to call, but Hybrid A’s YTC is considerably lower than its YTM, while Hybrid B trades at a lower price, with YTC much closer to its YTM. 

- This one difference gives rise to the very different convexity profile for the two hybrids. Note the ‘kink’ in the relationship that occurs when yields rise above ~5.75% for both of these securities. Below this level, the bond prices to a shorter-duration call, giving the price/yield relationship a gentle slope. Above this yield level, the bond would price to maturity, with much larger associated duration, and much more to lose with each incremental rise in yields  this increasingly severe downside illustrates negative convexity. 

## **Hybrids Trading to Call Date Generally Have Relatively Even Convexity Profiles**

## EXAMPLES ON THIS SLIDE ARE FOR ILLUSTRATIVE PURPOSES ONLY **HYBRID VALUATIONS How Can Investors Measure Ne ative Convexit ? g y** 

## **Upside/Downside Ratio** 

- The ratio of hybrid returns between a 100bp decrease and increase in yield. 

- The higher this ‘upside/downside ratio’, the better the convexity profile. 

- For trading to maturity, the price fall with a 100bp rise in yields would be much more severe than any potential gains from a 100bp fall in its yield; the upside/downside ratio would be less than 100%. 

## **Estimated Change in Duration** 

- For most perps trading to call, the change in duration should be minimal. For perps trading to maturity, however, or those with YTC close to YTW, the new duration could increase dramatically. 

## **YTC ∆ to Non-Call Kink** 

- Estimates the spread increases needed to bring the hybrid to that kink. The lower this spread, the closer a hybrid is to duration extension. 

- Let’s look at the choice that faces an issuer when its hybrid spread increases dramatically. As spread increases up to the ‘kink’, YTC is still lower than YTM, and it makes economic sense to call. As hybrid spreads continue to increase beyond the ‘kink’, YTW is lower than YTC, and it becomes uneconomical for the issuer to call. Hybrid starts trading to the second call, and duration extends.

## **Analyst Stock Ratings** 

Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.

**M O R G A N   S T A N L E Y   R E S E A R C H Corporate Hybrids Playbook** February 4, 2019 

## **Disclosure Section (Cont.)** 

Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months. 

Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months. 

Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months. 

Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months. 

## **Analyst Industry Views** 

Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. 

In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. 

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. 

Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.
